ParlaMint-AT_2007-12-05-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00041_d8e23732	Madam President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! There is no agreement of the Freedom Party on this subject, and we have discussed this briefly in the Committee. We think it is wrong from the principle that each department holds its own non-university research institute. We also have the legitimate suspicion, that this may perhaps be used as the so-called propaganda institution for the implementation of your purposes in the future debate on school policy. When I read through this draft law, I also conclude that, in the already customary agreement in the coalition, one would like to create a new institute on a site already working in this direction in Salzburg, but of course there will be two directors, then also nine members in a Supervisory Board and seven more members for the scientific advisory board and much more. In the end and in the long run, one will have to observe – such non-university research institutions somehow have to devour more money from year to year – that the whole will cost about € 6.5 million per year. Although applied by repainting, this is a considerable sum. I am not yet aware of what will improve in terms of quality. We have received the information that you want to award corresponding orders to scientists. I would have liked to see existing university institutes commissioned here with the studies or research you want to do. Why it should be a non-university research institute, you have remained guilty of this answer. It is quite legitimate for one to “hold” in one’s own department in the form of a non-university research institute – I say now. That's legitimate. Whether you want it politically or not is another question. We don't want it. We think that research in this field would have been better abolished at the Austrian universities and, in the sense of order research, it would have been possible to distribute the corresponding orders there. ParlaMint-AT_2007-12-05-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00041_d8e28543	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! I would just like to say shortly that the daylight, that is, a light which is significantly weaker than the passing light – a number of new cars already know this daylight, there are also a number of new cars that are equipped with their own switch system and can regulate the light if necessary – for the technical development of road safety from my point of view there is a great future for the European Union, as I would say as an optimist, which should then require the correct technical status as an obligation. The daylight is different from the glare light because it does not have the often criticized glare effect and also does not have the very often criticized consumption of extra petrol and thus the increased emissions of CO2. The function of the glare effect is not any theoretical, but actually an internationally determined by many experts who compared to one-track driving. It is proven that pedestrians, especially children, are suffering a disadvantage in road transport. Whether this should be applied to an accident statistics, I am personally careful because I am not anyone who believes that accidents are monocausal, and therefore I would not use the accident statistics directly for that. But that it is a disadvantage for single-track vehicles, that it is a disadvantage for children, for pedestrians, and that a glare effect in road transport is not the future, but it is this light of day, as it is discussed at European level, that is, I believe – with all the contradictions of the opinions – something like an undisputed opinion. Now we have not said that we do not place it under punishment or recommend not to turn the passing light on, but we have said that if it has so many disadvantages that have actually been proved, then it is not worth punishing someone who can be in good light conditions in January as in July, they can be as lunch as in the afternoon – it is decided not to turn the glare on. Therefore, as has been shown by the speakers, a large majority has decided on this measure. And I would also like to thank you for this discussion, because this issue has caused much confusion, even because of various expert opinions. In my view, it has never been planned to introduce this regulation and to leave it permanently uncontrolled and without evaluation, but it has always been a test phase, which I believe has the opportunity to weigh benefits and disadvantages against each other. Much has been said about the counting rule, including telephoneling at the wheel, for which I do not want to say anything, but only on the question of winter tyres. I think it is a good example in a democratic process when a Member makes a proposal in a discussion, as Helmut Kukacka has also made public, and we say that, together in the parliamentary process, we are able to discuss such a proposal. I personally think this is a good introduction. You should not believe that a law is good because it was in review. If there are proposals in the direction – which I very much welcome – that it is an improvement in the situation on the road when drivers are equipped with good tires or/and also carry snow chains, then it has to be picked up quickly. I particularly welcome this. If 95 percent of the drivers have the right tires, then on the motorway the problem is usually not with this 95 percent, but with the other 5 percent, especially with trucks. Especially in the case of trucks, a small percentage may also result in significant safety problems by either, as demonstrated in the checks of the trucks, not functioning brakes or the tire is wrong or the wrong chains have been brought along. Therefore, on the one hand, there is a legal provision as to how we create it today, including the addition that has arisen in the parliamentary process, and on the other hand, control closely together. I would never argue that laws and control do not belong together, because laws can exercise a certain role model effect, but if the control does not take place, then this model effect does not last long. Public relations on the directive of a new law can only be taken seriously in connection with control. I would therefore like to thank all those who have participated constructively in this discussion. Finally, I would like to join those who thank Kurt Eder for the good cooperation. Mr Eder is an example of constructive and objective discussion and has always been an example in the discussion of transport safety and transport policy. Thank you.
